Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I get angry. I try very hard not to be a bitter sort of person, Doctor. I really don't hold any grudges, it's just not in my nature.

I hope it's alright that I don't get angry about it... I just think it's better to let sleeping dogs lie and not make a fuss. Am I doing the right thing by just brushing it off like that?

Oh, no, Doctor. No, I really don't. I mean... sure, everyone has a quiet afternoon here and there, but feeling sad or hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that at all.

So, no, I'm generally quite content with my days. I hope that's the right way to go about it? Just staying positive and not dwelling on gloomy things... that's healthy, isn't it?

Does that sound like what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you avoid your workshop or family gatherings, is it because you’re worried that you might have a panic attack or experience intense anxiety in those places?\n\nPatient: Well, goodness no, Doctor. I wouldn't say I'm worried about having a... a panic attack, or anything dramatic like that. I'm not sitting around afraid I'm going to lose my head or make a scene. I hope it doesn't sound like I'm being difficult, but it's really not like that at all. \n\nI just prefer to stay away because, well, it just brings up things I'd rather not think about. It's more about just keeping my peace of mind, you know? Just quietly keeping to myself so I don't get bothered by it all. I'm certainly not scared of having a fit. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer to give... I just want to be sure I'm explaining myself properly so I don't waste your time. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about what specifically in the workshop or about your brother-in-law makes you want to avoid those situations?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit embarrassing to admit, to be honest. I don't want you to think I'm dwelling on things or... or losing my grip. But sometimes, just out of nowhere, I'll... I'll see his hand. With that heavy tool. \n\nIt's like a picture that just pops right into my head when I'm not even trying to think about it. I could just be shaving, or working on a little painting, and there it is. And I have to admit, it still makes my chest tighten up a bit when it happens. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too strange, Doctor. I really try to just push it out of my mind and keep busy. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you see that image of your brother-in-law's hand with the tool, does it feel like you're reliving a past event, or is it more like just remembering something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really just a memory, Doctor. I wouldn't say I'm reliving it, no. That sounds a bit... well, a bit extreme, doesn't it? Like I'm losing touch with reality or something. I assure you, I know exactly where I am and what year it is when it happens. \n\nIt's just an unpleasant memory that flashes by, like remembering a bad fall or... or dropping a nice piece of china. It's just a quick picture, and then I shake my head and go back to what I was doing. I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, because I really am doing quite well otherwise. \n\nI hope it's okay to put it that way. I just want to be sure I'm not giving you the wrong idea about how I'm handling things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned seeing that image of your brother-in-law's hand, do you find yourself feeling angry, fearful, or upset when those memories come up?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I get angry. I try very hard not to be a bitter sort of person, Doctor. I really don't hold any grudges, it's just not in my nature. \n\nAnd as for being fearful or upset... well, maybe I feel just a tiny bit startled for a split second, sort of like when a door slams unexpectedly? But I certainly don't get all worked up or emotional about it. I'm not sitting around crying or shaking my fist, I promise you that. \n\nI just take a quiet breath and go right back to my crossword or my painting. I try to keep a pleasant attitude, you know, so I'm not a bother to anyone around me. I hope it's alright that I don't get angry about it... I just think it's better to let sleeping dogs lie and not make a fuss. Am I doing the right thing by just brushing it off like that?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you find that you’re less interested in things you used to enjoy, or that you don’t feel pleasure in activities the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor, I wouldn't say that at all. Well, I... I suppose I do feel a little bit of guilt sometimes, yes. I try not to dwell on it, of course, because I know it doesn't do anyone any good to wallow. But... when that image pops into my head, I... I just keep telling myself I should have put my foot down sooner.

I just want to be sure I'm not giving you the wrong idea about how I'm handling things. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say I avoid it. I mean, I certainly don't try to shut off my feelings or anything like that. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? 

I just... well, if the thought pops up, I try to acknowledge it and then just gently move on to something more productive. I don't want to sit around brooding over it, because I know my wife needs me to be steady. I just try to keep myself busy with chores around the house or reading a good book. 

I'm not running away from it, I promise. I just prefer to focus on the positive things in front of me instead of getting all tangled up in bad memories. That's the right way to handle these things, isn't it? Just keeping a stiff upper lip and carrying on?

emotionally numb or detached from people, activities, or parts of your life that used to mean something to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. With that heavy tool. \n\nIt's like a picture that just pops right into my head when I'm not even trying to think about it. I could just be shaving, or working on a little painting, and there it is. And I have to admit, it still makes my chest tighten up a bit when it happens. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too strange, Doctor. I really try to just push it out of my mind and keep busy. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you see that image of your brother-in-law's hand with the tool, does it feel like you're reliving a past event, or is it more like just remembering something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really just a memory, Doctor. I wouldn't say I'm reliving it, no. That sounds a bit... well, a bit extreme, doesn't it? Like I'm losing touch with reality or something. It's just a quick picture, and then I shake my head and go back to what I was doing. I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, because I really am doing quite well otherwise. \n\nI hope it's okay to put it that way. I just want to be sure I'm not giving you the wrong idea about how I'm handling things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned seeing that image of your brother-in-law's hand, do you find yourself feeling angry, fearful, or upset when those memories come up?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I get angry. I try very hard not to be a bitter sort of person, Doctor. I really don't hold any grudges, it's just not in my nature. \n\nAnd as for being fearful or upset... well, maybe I feel just a tiny bit startled for a split second, sort of like when a door slams unexpectedly? But I certainly don't get all worked up or emotional about it. I'm not sitting around crying or shaking my fist, I promise you that. \n\nI just take a quiet breath and go right back to my crossword or my painting. I try to keep a pleasant attitude, you know, so I'm not a bother to anyone around me. I hope it's alright that I don't get angry about it... I just think it's better to let sleeping dogs lie and not make a fuss. Am I doing the right thing by just brushing it off like that?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you find that you’re less interested in things you used to enjoy, or that you don’t feel pleasure in activities the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor, I wouldn't say that at all. I still very much enjoy my little routines. Like I mentioned before, my painting and my crosswords... they still bring me quite a bit of joy, really. \n\nI think it's so important to keep busy, don't you? I just want to make sure I'm doing all the right things to keep my spirits up and stay active.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor. No, I wouldn't say that at all. \n\nI try very hard to look on the bright side of things. I count my blessings, you know? I think it's just so important to keep a cheerful disposition. Nobody likes a sourpuss, and I'd just hate to bring down the mood for my family or the neighbors. I'd just feel terrible if anyone ever thought I was giving them the cold shoulder or pulling away from them. I try to keep a pleasant attitude, you know, so I'm not a bother to anyone around me. I hope it's alright that I don't get angry about it... I just think it's better to let sleeping dogs lie and not make a fuss. Am I doing the right thing by just brushing it off like that?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you find that you’re less interested in things you used to enjoy, or that you don’t feel pleasure in activities the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor, I wouldn't say that at all. I still very much enjoy my little routines. Like I mentioned before, my painting and my crosswords... they still bring me quite a bit of joy, really. \n\nI think it's so important to keep busy, don't you? I certainly wouldn't want to just sit around the house moping all day—I imagine that would be quite a burden on the people around me, and I really try my best not to be a bother to anyone. So, yes, I still find plenty of pleasure in my day-to-day activities. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? I just want to make sure I'm doing all the right things to keep my spirits up and stay active.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor. No, I really don't. I mean... sure, everyone has a quiet afternoon here and there, but feeling sad or hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that at all. \n\nI try very hard to look on the bright side of things. I count my blessings, you know? I think it's just so important to keep a cheerful disposition. Nobody likes a sourpuss, and I'd just hate to bring down the mood for my family or the neighbors. I always try to put on a smile and make the best of things. \n\nSo, no, I'm generally quite content with my days. I hope that's the right way to go about it? Just staying positive and not dwelling on gloomy things... that's healthy, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel emotionally numb or detached from people, activities, or parts of your life that used to mean something to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I'd just feel terrible if anyone ever thought I was giving them the cold shoulder or pulling away from them. That just wouldn't be polite at all. \n\nI still feel very connected to the folks in my life and the things I do every day. I make it a point to check in on people and really listen, you know? I think it's so important to stay engaged and show people you care, don't you agree? I certainly wouldn't want to become one of those cold, distant types... I hope it's okay that I still feel so attached to everything? I mean, I certainly don't try to shut off my feelings or anything like that. That wouldn't be very healthy, would it? \n\nI just... well, if the thought pops up, I try to acknowledge it and then just gently move on to something more productive. I just try to keep myself busy with chores around the house or reading a good book. \n\nI'm not running away from it, I promise. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, guilt? Or shame? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I certainly don't sit around feeling ashamed of myself or anything like that. That wouldn't do anyone any good, would it?

I mean, sure, sometimes I think about how I could have... well, maybe smoothed things over better back then. But it's not a heavy guilt or anything that weighs me down. Once I get back to my book or fixing something around the house, I feel perfectly fine. At peace, just like you said. 

I try to be a good husband, a good man. I don't let myself get bogged down in those kinds of negative feelings. It's better to just look forward, right? I'm doing the right thing by just staying positive and moving on, aren't I?

I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers here... did that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel uneasy or anxious about being in places or situations where it might be hard for you to leave quickly or where you feel trapped?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I feel trapped or anything like that. I hope it doesn't sound like I'm being difficult, but it's really not like that at all. \n\nI just prefer to stay away because, well, it just brings up things I'd rather not think about. It's more about just keeping my peace of mind, you know? Just quietly keeping to myself so I don't get bothered by it all. I'm certainly not scared of having a fit. \n\nI hope that's an alright answer to give... I just want to be sure I'm explaining myself properly so I don't waste your time. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about what specifically in the workshop or about your brother-in-law makes you want to avoid those situations?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a bit embarrassing to admit, to be honest. With that heavy tool. \n\nIt's like a picture that just pops right into my head when I'm not even trying to think about it. I could just be shaving, or working on a little painting, and there it is. It's just a quick picture, and then I shake my head and go back to what I was doing. I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, because I really am doing quite well otherwise. \n\nI hope it's okay to put it that way. I just want to be sure I'm not giving you the wrong idea about how I'm handling things. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since you mentioned seeing that image of your brother-in-law's hand, do you find yourself feeling angry, fearful, or upset when those memories come up?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I get angry. I try very hard not to be a bitter sort of person, Doctor. I really don't hold any grudges, it's just not in my nature. \n\nAnd as for being fearful or upset... well, maybe I feel just a tiny bit startled for a split second, sort of like when a door slams unexpectedly? But I certainly don't get all worked up or emotional about it. I'm not sitting around crying or shaking my fist, I promise you that. \n\nI just take a quiet breath and go right back to my crossword or my painting. I try to keep a pleasant attitude, you know, so I'm not a bother to anyone around me. I hope it's alright that I don't get angry about it... I just think it's better to let sleeping dogs lie and not make a fuss. Am I doing the right thing by just brushing it off like that?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you find that you’re less interested in things you used to enjoy, or that you don’t feel pleasure in activities the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor, I wouldn't say that at all. I still very much enjoy my little routines. Like I mentioned before, my painting and my crosswords... they still bring me quite a bit of joy, really. \n\nI think it's so important to keep busy, don't you? I certainly wouldn't want to just sit around the house moping all day—I imagine that would be quite a burden on the people around me, and I really try my best not to be a bother to anyone. So, yes, I still find plenty of pleasure in my day-to-day activities. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? I just want to make sure I'm doing all the right things to keep my spirits up and stay active.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling down, sad, or hopeless for most of the day, more days than not, even if just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, Doctor. No, I really don't. I mean... sure, everyone has a quiet afternoon here and there, but feeling sad or hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that at all. \n\nI try very hard to look on the bright side of things. I count my blessings, you know? I think it's just so important to keep a cheerful disposition. Nobody likes a sourpuss, and I'd just hate to bring down the mood for my family or the neighbors. I always try to put on a smile and make the best of things. \n\nSo, no, I'm generally quite content with my days. I hope that's the right way to go about it? Just staying positive and not dwelling on gloomy things... that's healthy, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Do you ever feel emotionally numb or detached from people, activities, or parts of your life that used to mean something to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. Numb? No, I really don't think so. I care very deeply for my family and my friends. I try to be as warm and present as I possibly can be. I'd just feel terrible if anyone ever thought I was giving them the cold shoulder or pulling away from them. That just wouldn't be polite at all. \n\nI still feel very connected to the folks in my life and the things I do every day. I make it a point to check in on people and really listen, you know? I think it's so important to stay engaged and show people you care, don't you agree? I certainly wouldn't want to become one of those cold, distant types... I hope it's okay that I still feel so attached to everything? I just try to be a good, supportive person to have around.\n\nDoctor: When you see that image of your brother-in-law's hand, do you ever feel guilt, shame, or a sense that something bad is your fault?\n\nPatient: Well, I... Well, I... I wouldn't say I think I can't handle things. I mean, I've managed my household for years, right? I'm a capable man. 

But... well, I suppose sometimes, just occasionally, I do catch myself thinking that maybe... maybe I should have just put my foot down sooner. You know, when he first asked to stay with us. I just want to make sure I'm being honest with you, but I don't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself. That's just a normal way to look at a mistake, right?
